# OCR GCSE Combined Science B Twenty First Century (J260)

Complete notes for this qualification, generated from `curriculum/notes` by `curriculum/scripts/compile.py`. Do not edit this file; edit the notes and run the script again. Spec references are not yet checked by a person (`refs_checked: false`).

Order and contents follow `notes/gcse/ocr-b-sciences/map-j260.md`. 30 notes files.

## Spec map

# OCR Twenty First Century Science Combined Science B (J260): spec map

Every assessable learning outcome of OCR GCSE (9–1) Combined Science B (Twenty First Century Science), J260, in OCR's order, with the notes that cover it. Biology B1 to B6, chemistry C1 to C6 and physics P1 to P6, plus Ideas about Science. Combined Science B renumbers some sub-topics and outcomes (for example its B2.5 is J257's B2.6), so each J260 outcome was matched to the same outcome in J257, J258 or J259 and uses that outcome's notes. There are sixteen combined PAGs (B1 to B5, C1 to C5, P1 to P6).

The links go to the AQA GCSE notes in `notes/aqa/gcse/` (the section named in each link) or to the gap files in this folder, which hold only what the AQA notes lack. Where a spec point is wider than one AQA section, every section it needs is listed.

How to read the table:

- **Ref**: OCR's learning outcome reference, for example B1.1.1 (sub-topic B1.1, outcome 1). The topic and sub-topic come from the reference.
- **Sub-topic**: OCR's sub-topic heading.
- **Flags**: HT = Higher tier only (OCR prints these statements in bold). 
- **IaS**: Ideas about Science outcomes (IaS1.1 to IaS4.6), taught through every chapter. They are the same in all four OCR B specifications and are covered once, in [ideas-about-science.md](ideas-about-science.md).
- **Practical activity groups (PAGs)**: OCR's eight groups of practical apparatus and techniques for this subject, listed at the end. Each links to the practical guide in this folder and to the AQA required practical that uses the same techniques.

`refs_checked: false`: references, tiers and the separate-only flags were taken from the OCR specification PDF (Version 5.0, June 2026) and still need a person to check them. Separate-only flags were found by matching each outcome against the Combined Science B specification. Sub-topic titles are OCR's headings; nothing else of OCR's wording is reproduced.

Gap files:

- [ideas-about-science.md](ideas-about-science.md): IaS1 to IaS4.
- [storyline-contexts.md](storyline-contexts.md): genetic testing, identifying the cause of an infection, reducing air pollution, PET recycling, sampling and qualitative analysis, titration data quality.
- [../ocr-a-sciences/biology-contexts.md](../ocr-a-sciences/biology-contexts.md), [../ocr-a-sciences/chemistry-contexts.md](../ocr-a-sciences/chemistry-contexts.md) and [../ocr-a-sciences/practical-activity-groups.md](../ocr-a-sciences/practical-activity-groups.md): content shared with OCR Gateway (named diseases, ATP, sampling, empirical formulae, PAGs). Their front matter lists the OCR B references too.

## Ideas about Science

OCR's Twenty First Century Science specifications have a chapter called Ideas about Science (IaS). It is the same in Biology B, Chemistry B, Physics B and Combined Science B. It covers how scientists plan investigations, handle data, develop explanations, and how science affects society. There is no separate paper: questions on these ideas are set in the context of any topic, so practise them with real examples from every chapter.

### Overview

The four groups are:

- IaS1: what to think about when planning an investigation (8 outcomes)
- IaS2: what conclusions can be drawn from data (12 outcomes)
- IaS3: how scientific explanations are developed (4 outcomes)
- IaS4: how science and technology affect society (6 outcomes).

#### Board differences

- Only OCR B has an Ideas about Science chapter. OCR Gateway and AQA examine the same skills as "Working scientifically", listed separately from the subject content, so their maps do not list them as spec points.
- In J260 the IaS2 heading is worded slightly differently from J257 to J259; the outcomes are the same.

### IaS1 Planning an investigation

*Refs: OCR B J257/J258/J259/J260 IaS1.1 to IaS1.8.*

#### What you need to know

- Use a scientific theory to write a **hypothesis** (a testable explanation) and a **prediction** (what you expect to observe if the hypothesis is right), and justify them.
- Choose apparatus and techniques that give **accurate**, **precise** and **valid** data, and explain the choice.
- Know the scientific quantities you are measuring and how each is found (for example, rate = change ÷ time; density = mass ÷ volume).
- Identify the **independent** variable (changed), the **dependent** variable (measured) and the **control** variables (kept the same), and say how each control variable is kept the same.
- Choose a sensible **sample size** and **range** and number of values, and justify them.
- Plan a clear, ordered method to make observations, make or identify a substance, test a hypothesis, collect and check data, or explore something new.
- Identify **hazards** and say how to reduce the risk.
- Communicate the plan with correct terms, diagrams, tables, graphs, numbers and symbols.

#### Explained

**Hypothesis and prediction.** A hypothesis explains; a prediction describes an outcome. Example: *Hypothesis*: enzymes work faster when warmer because particles collide more often with more energy, until the enzyme denatures. *Prediction*: the time for starch to disappear will fall as temperature rises from 20 °C to 40 °C, then rise again above about 50 °C.

**Key words about data quality.**

| Term | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Accurate | Close to the true value |
| Precise | Repeated measurements are close together (little spread) |
| Repeatable | The same person, method and equipment give similar results |
| Reproducible | A different person, method or equipment gives similar results |
| Valid | The method measures what it claims to, and only the independent variable affects the result |
| Resolution | The smallest change an instrument can show |

**Choosing apparatus.** A measuring cylinder with 1 cm³ divisions is fine for 50 cm³ of acid in a rate experiment, but a burette or pipette is needed in a titration. A gas syringe gives more precise gas volumes than counting bubbles. A digital thermometer reading to 0.1 °C is better than a liquid thermometer reading to 1 °C if temperature changes are small.

**Controlling variables.** Say how, not just what: "keep temperature the same by using a water bath at 30 °C", "use the same mass of magnesium ribbon by weighing 0.10 g on a balance".

**Sample size and range.** Bigger samples reduce the effect of random variation and unusual individuals, but cost more time and money. In biology, a sample must be big enough to be representative (for example at least 10 quadrats). A range should cover enough values to show the pattern, with at least five values, and include extra readings where the graph changes most.

**Hazard and risk.** A hazard is something that could cause harm (concentrated acid, a Bunsen flame, a hot ray box). The risk is the chance of harm happening and how bad it would be. Reduce risk with eye protection, smaller volumes, lower concentrations, heat-proof mats, tied-back hair, and by handling microorganisms with aseptic technique.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing a prediction with no reason, when the question asks you to justify it using a theory.
- Saying "a fair test" without naming which variables are controlled and how.
- Mixing up accurate and precise. A set of readings can be precise (close together) but inaccurate (all wrong in the same way, such as a balance that was not zeroed).
- Listing hazards without saying how to reduce the risk.

### IaS2 Processing, presenting and evaluating data

*Refs: OCR B J257/J258/J259/J260 IaS2.1 to IaS2.12.*

#### What you need to know

- Present data in suitable tables (independent variable in the first column, units in the headings) and charts.
- Use SI units and IUPAC chemical names; use prefixes (tera, giga, mega, kilo, centi, milli, micro, nano) and powers of ten.
- Convert data between forms (table to graph, graph to words) and between units.
- Give answers to a sensible number of **significant figures** (no more than the data).
- Choose the right graph, label axes with quantity and unit, use sensible scales, plot accurately, draw a line or curve of best fit, and show uncertainty with **range bars**.
- Find patterns and trends, calculate the **mean** and **range**, read values from a graph, find a **gradient**, and use **interpolation** and **extrapolation**.
- Judge data for accuracy, precision, repeatability and reproducibility; spot **random** and **systematic errors**; decide whether to keep or reject an **outlier**.
- Evaluate a method, suggest improvements, and explain how they improve the data; suggest further investigations.
- Draw conclusions from data, using correct scientific terms, and explain how far the data support or contradict a hypothesis.

#### Explained

**Prefixes.**

| Prefix | Symbol | Factor |
| --- | --- | --- |
| tera | T | 10¹² |
| giga | G | 10⁹ |
| mega | M | 10⁶ |
| kilo | k | 10³ |
| centi | c | 10⁻² |
| milli | m | 10⁻³ |
| micro | µ | 10⁻⁶ |
| nano | n | 10⁻⁹ |

**Choosing a graph.** Bar chart when the independent variable is categoric (types of metal, blood groups). Line graph or scatter graph when both variables are continuous (temperature, time). Histogram for grouped continuous data (heights of plants in classes).

**Mean, range and outliers.** The mean is the total divided by the number of values. The range is the largest minus the smallest value; range bars on a graph show the spread of repeats. An **outlier** is a value that does not fit the pattern. Check it first: if there is a known reason (a misread scale, a leak), leave it out of the mean and say why. If there is no reason, it may be real, so repeat the measurement rather than just discard it.

**Errors.**

- *Random errors* make readings scatter unpredictably around the true value (for example, reaction time when using a stopwatch). Reduce their effect by repeating and taking a mean.
- *Systematic errors* shift all readings the same way (a balance reading 0.2 g with nothing on it; always reading a meniscus from above). Repeats do not help; fix the cause or correct the readings.
- A *zero error* is a systematic error where an instrument does not read zero when it should.

**Graphs.** The gradient of a straight line = change in y ÷ change in x, with units (for a distance–time graph the gradient is speed in m/s). For a curve, draw a tangent and find its gradient. Interpolation reads between plotted points; extrapolation extends the line beyond them, which is less reliable because the pattern may change.

**Evaluating and concluding.** A conclusion says what the pattern is and explains it with science; it must not go beyond the range tested. Confidence in a hypothesis increases when results match its prediction, are repeatable and reproducible, and have little spread. One set of results that agrees cannot prove a hypothesis; a reliable result that disagrees can show that it needs changing.

#### Worked example

A student times how long magnesium takes to react with acid at five temperatures. At 40 °C the repeats are 38 s, 41 s and 59 s.

- The 59 s reading is an outlier. If no reason is known, repeat the measurement. Here the student found the ribbon was folded, so it is excluded.
- Mean = (38 + 41) ÷ 2 = 39.5 s, given as 40 s (2 significant figures, as in the data).
- Range of the remaining repeats = 41 − 38 = 3 s.

Unit conversion: 250 cm³ = 0.25 dm³ = 2.5 × 10⁻⁴ m³. A cell 20 µm across is 2.0 × 10⁻⁵ m.

Gradient: a straight line on a distance–time graph passes through (2 s, 6 m) and (8 s, 24 m). Gradient = (24 − 6) ÷ (8 − 2) = 18 ÷ 6 = 3 m/s.

#### Common mistakes

- Giving a mean to more significant figures than the data, such as 39.6667 s.
- Joining points dot-to-dot instead of drawing a smooth line or curve of best fit.
- Putting units in every cell of a table instead of in the column heading.
- Saying "repeat to make it more accurate". Repeating improves precision and helps spot outliers; it does not remove systematic error.
- Extrapolating far beyond the data as if it were certain.

### IaS3 Developing scientific explanations

*Refs: OCR B J257/J258/J259/J260 IaS3.1 to IaS3.4.*

#### What you need to know

- Spot a **correlation** in text, a table or a graph, and tell the difference between a correlation and a cause.
- Explain why a factor can increase the chance of an outcome without always causing it.
- Explain why one person's case is not good evidence for or against a correlation.
- Know that a correlation is more likely to be a cause when there is a plausible **mechanism** that explains it.
- Describe examples of scientific theories that changed when new evidence appeared.
- Describe **peer review**: other scientists in the field check work before it is published.
- Use models (representational, spatial, descriptive, computational, mathematical) to solve problems, make predictions and explain ideas, and identify their limitations.

#### Explained

**Correlation and cause.** A correlation means two variables change together. A cause means changing one actually changes the other. Ice cream sales and sunburn are correlated, but ice cream does not cause sunburn; both are caused by sunny weather (a third factor). Smoking and lung cancer are correlated, and smoking is accepted as a cause because large studies controlled for other factors and a mechanism was found: chemicals in smoke damage DNA in lung cells.

**Risk factors.** A risk factor increases the chance of an outcome but does not make it certain. Many smokers never get lung cancer, and some non-smokers do. One case ("my grandfather smoked and lived to 95") does not disprove the correlation, because the evidence is about large groups.

**Theories change.** Examples from the chapters:

- Models of the atom: Dalton's solid spheres, Thomson's plum pudding, Rutherford's nuclear model (after the alpha-scattering results), Bohr's electron shells.
- Mendeleev's periodic table, accepted when the elements he predicted were discovered.
- Germ theory replacing the idea that disease came from bad air.
- Darwin's natural selection, later supported by genetics.

**Peer review.** Before a paper is published in a journal, other experts check the method, data and conclusions. They may ask for changes or reject it. Peer review reduces errors and fraud but does not guarantee a result is right; other scientists still need to repeat the work.

**Models.** Every model simplifies. The particle model explains states of matter but treats particles as solid spheres; ball-and-stick models show bonds but not their real lengths; a computer climate model can predict but depends on its starting data and assumptions. Saying what a model leaves out is part of using it well.

#### Common mistakes

- Concluding "A causes B" from a correlation alone.
- Saying peer review proves a claim is true.
- Describing a model without giving any limitation when the question asks for one.

### IaS4 Science, technology and society

*Refs: OCR B J257/J258/J259/J260 IaS4.1 to IaS4.6.*

#### What you need to know

- Give examples of science that has greatly improved people's lives, and of risks that new technology has caused.
- For a situation, identify who benefits and who takes the risks, discuss what should be done, explain why people accept some risks, and tell the difference between **perceived risk** and **calculated risk**.
- Explain why different people, places or countries may sensibly make different decisions on the same issue (personal, social, economic and environmental reasons), and make a decision based on evidence.
- Tell the difference between questions science can answer and questions it cannot. Where an ethical issue is involved, state it clearly and summarise the different views.
- Explain why scientists communicate their work to different audiences.

#### Explained

**Benefits and risks.** Vaccines, antibiotics, clean water treatment, electricity and fertilisers have transformed health and food supply. New technologies also brought risks: CFCs damaged the ozone layer, overuse of antibiotics led to resistant bacteria, burning fossil fuels is changing the climate.

**Calculated and perceived risk.** Calculated risk comes from data: the number of times something happened divided by the number of times it could have happened (for example deaths per million journeys). Perceived risk is how risky people feel something is. People usually feel risks are bigger when they are new, unfamiliar, imposed on them, or could cause a disaster (nuclear power, flying), and smaller when they are familiar, voluntary or give a benefit they value (driving, sunbathing).

**Different decisions.** A country with plenty of sunshine and space may choose large solar farms; another may choose wind or nuclear power. A wealthy country may ban a cheap pesticide that a poorer country still needs to protect its food supply. Both decisions can be sensible in their own context.

**What science can and cannot answer.** Science can answer "Does this gene variant increase the risk of a disease?" It cannot decide "Should parents be allowed to choose embryos without that variant?", because that depends on values. For an ethical question, say what the issue is (for example, the rights of an embryo against the wish to prevent suffering) and give the main views on each side.

**Communicating science.** Scientists share results so other scientists can check and build on them (journals, conferences), so the public and decision-makers can make informed choices (news, reports), and because much research is publicly funded. The language and detail are changed to suit the audience.

#### Worked example

In a year, 1 800 people out of a town of 600 000 were injured cycling. The calculated risk = 1 800 ÷ 600 000 = 0.003, or 3 in 1 000 people per year. If most people in the town believe cycling is far more dangerous than travelling by car, their perceived risk does not match the data, which is something to check before making a decision.

#### Common mistakes

- Giving only benefits or only risks when asked to evaluate.
- Treating an ethical question as if an experiment could settle it.
- Saying a decision is "wrong" because another country chose differently, without considering its context.

### Check questions

1. A student investigates how the concentration of sugar solution affects the change in mass of potato cylinders. Name the independent variable, the dependent variable and two control variables. [4 marks]
2. Explain the difference between precise and accurate results. [2 marks]
3. Convert 0.045 kg to grams, and 3.2 mm to micrometres. [2 marks]
4. Repeat readings of a reaction time are 0.31 s, 0.28 s, 0.30 s and 0.52 s. Identify the outlier, then calculate the mean and the range of the other readings. [3 marks]
5. A balance reads 0.3 g when nothing is on it. Name this type of error and explain why repeating the readings would not remove it. [3 marks]
6. Data show that people who carry a lighter are more likely to get lung cancer. Explain why this does not show that lighters cause lung cancer. [2 marks]
7. Describe what happens in peer review and give one reason it is useful. [3 marks]
8. Give one strength and one limitation of the particle model of matter. [2 marks]
9. A new medicine reduces the chance of a heart attack but has rare serious side effects. Suggest why some patients accept the risk and others do not. [3 marks]
10. Give one question about genetic testing that science can answer and one that it cannot, and explain why. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Independent: concentration of sugar solution (1). Dependent: change in mass of the cylinders (1). Any two controls: length / diameter / starting mass of cylinders; volume of solution; time left in solution; temperature; type of potato (2).
2. Precise: repeat readings are close to each other / small spread (1). Accurate: close to the true value (1).
3. 45 g (1); 3200 µm (1).
4. 0.52 s is the outlier (1). Mean = (0.31 + 0.28 + 0.30) ÷ 3 = 0.30 s (1). Range = 0.31 − 0.28 = 0.03 s (1).
5. Zero error / systematic error (1). Every reading is 0.3 g too high (1); the mean of repeats is still 0.3 g too high, so the balance must be zeroed or 0.3 g subtracted (1).
6. Correlation does not show cause (1); a third factor (smoking) explains both, as smokers carry lighters (1).
7. Before publication, other experts in the same field (1) check the methods, data and conclusions (1). Useful because it finds errors / reduces fraud / makes published work more trustworthy (1).
8. Strength: explains changes of state / density / gas pressure / diffusion (1). Limitation: treats particles as solid spheres / ignores forces between particles / does not show spaces to scale (1).
9. Any three: patients at high risk of a heart attack gain a larger benefit; the side effect is rare (low calculated risk); people differ in how they perceive risk; personal experience (for example, a relative had a heart attack); some prefer lifestyle changes.
10. Can answer: whether a test correctly detects a gene variant / how likely a variant is to cause a disease (1). Cannot answer: whether people should be tested / whether embryos should be selected (1). Because that depends on values and ethics, not on evidence that can be measured (1).

<!-- source: notes/gcse/ocr-b-sciences/storyline-contexts.md -->

## OCR B storyline contexts

OCR's Twenty First Century Science courses teach each chapter as a story built around a question, such as "How can we identify the cause of an infection?" or "What happens to products at the end of their useful life?". Most of the science is the same as in the AQA notes, but a few outcomes use contexts that AQA does not cover. This file covers only those: genetic testing, how infections are identified, ways of cutting air pollution, PET bottle recycling and recycling decisions, representative sampling, qualitative and quantitative analysis, and judging the quality of titration data.

### Overview

#### Board differences

- J257 (Biology B): B1.3.1 (genetic testing and the human genome in medicine), B2.4.1 (detecting and identifying diseases; separate science only).
- J258 (Chemistry B): C1.1.8 (reducing pollutant emissions), C4.5.6 and C4.5.7 (PET recycling, recycling decisions), C5.2.1 (representative sampling; separate science only), C5.4.1 (qualitative and quantitative analysis; separate science only), C5.4.8 (titration data quality).
- J260 (Combined Science B) numbers the same outcomes differently: B1.3.1, C1.1.8, C4.4.5 and C4.4.6 (recycling), C5.3.7 (titration data quality).
- The related AQA sections are Biology 4.6.1.4, 4.6.1.7, 4.3.3.1 and 4.1.1.6; Chemistry 4.9.3, 4.10.2, 4.8 and 4.4.2.5.

### Genetic testing and the human genome in medicine

*Refs: OCR B J257/J260 B1.3.1.*

#### What you need to know

- Sequencing the human genome lets scientists find **alleles** (gene variants) linked to diseases.
- **Genetic testing** looks for particular alleles in a person's DNA, from a sample of blood, saliva or cells.
- Testing can inform **family planning** (for example, whether a couple could pass on an inherited disorder) and **healthcare** (for example, choosing the medicine most likely to work for that person).
- Discuss benefits, risks and ethical issues.

#### Explained

**Uses of genetic testing.**

- *Carrier testing*: a person with no symptoms finds out whether they carry one copy of a recessive allele, such as the allele for cystic fibrosis. If both partners are carriers, each child has a 1 in 4 chance of having the disorder.
- *Embryo and fetal testing*: cells from an embryo made by IVF, or from a fetus, are tested (embryo screening is in Biology 4.6.1.7).
- *Diagnostic testing*: confirms a diagnosis in someone who already has symptoms.
- *Predictive testing*: shows whether a person has an allele that raises their risk of a disease later in life, such as some alleles linked to breast cancer, so they can have more frequent check-ups or preventive treatment.
- *Testing before prescribing*: some alleles change how a person's body breaks down a medicine, so a test can show which drug or dose will work best and which may cause side effects.

**Limits.** Most diseases depend on many genes and on lifestyle and environment. Finding a risk allele usually raises the chance of a disease; it does not make it certain. Tests can also give false positives and false negatives.

**Issues to discuss.**

- Benefits: early treatment or monitoring; informed choices about having children; better-targeted medicines.
- Risks and concerns: anxiety from a result that may never lead to illness; information about relatives who did not choose to be tested; possible pressure to end a pregnancy; whether insurers or employers could use the results; who should store and see genetic data.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a genetic test shows that a person "will get" a disease. Usually it shows a changed risk.
- Forgetting that a test result also gives information about the person's parents, siblings and children.

### Identifying the cause of an infection

*Refs: OCR B J257 B2.4.1 (separate science only).*

#### What you need to know

- Describe ways diseases, including plant diseases, are detected and identified, in the lab and in the field.
- Describe how to use a light microscope to observe microorganisms.

#### Explained

To choose a treatment, doctors and plant scientists must know which pathogen is causing a disease. Methods include:

- **Observing symptoms**: many diseases have typical signs (a rash, a fever pattern, mosaic patterns on leaves, galls on stems). In the field, plant growers compare symptoms with guides and photographs.
- **Taking samples**: blood, urine, swabs, tissue, or (for plants) infected leaves, stems or soil.
- **Cell counts**: a raised number of white blood cells suggests an infection.
- **Culturing**: the sample is spread on agar so the pathogen grows into colonies that can be studied (aseptic technique is in Biology 4.1.1.6).
- **Microscopy and staining**: stains make bacteria visible and show their shape; a common stain sorts bacteria into two groups by their cell walls.
- **Testing with antimicrobials**: paper discs soaked in different antibiotics are placed on a culture. A clear zone shows the antibiotic kills or stops the bacterium; the bigger the zone, the more effective it is. This also shows which antibiotic to prescribe.
- **Genome analysis**: DNA from the sample is compared with known pathogen DNA. This identifies the pathogen quickly and exactly, even ones that are hard to grow.
- **Antibody-based tests**: testing kits use monoclonal antibodies that bind only to one pathogen (in Biology 4.3.2.2 and 4.3.3.1); some work in the field.
- **Isolation and reinfection**: the suspected pathogen is grown on its own (isolated), then used to infect a healthy organism (usually a plant or a culture of cells); if the same disease appears, that pathogen is the cause.

**Observing microorganisms with a light microscope.**

1. Using aseptic technique, place a small drop of culture on a clean slide and spread it thin.
2. Let it dry, then pass the slide briefly through a flame to fix the cells to the glass (this also kills them).
3. Add a stain (such as methylene blue) for about a minute, then rinse gently and blot dry.
4. Start with the lowest-power objective, focus with the coarse focus, then move to the highest power and use only the fine focus.

Bacteria are only about 1–5 µm long, so they appear as very small dots, rods or spirals even at the highest power. Viruses are far too small to see with a light microscope.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying viruses can be seen with a school light microscope.
- Saying a bigger clear zone means the bacteria are more resistant. It means the antibiotic is more effective (the bacteria are less resistant).
- Using the coarse focus at high power, which can crack the slide.

### Reducing air pollution from fuels

*Refs: OCR B J258/J260 C1.1.8.*

#### What you need to know

- The problems caused by carbon monoxide, particulates, sulfur dioxide and oxides of nitrogen are in Chemistry 4.9.3.2.
- Describe ways to cut these emissions, including **catalytic converters**, **low-sulfur petrol** (and diesel) and **gas scrubbers**.

#### Explained

| Method | How it works | Pollutants reduced |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Catalytic converter | Exhaust gases pass over a hot honeycomb coated with metal catalysts (platinum, palladium, rhodium). Carbon monoxide is oxidised to carbon dioxide; oxides of nitrogen are reduced to nitrogen; unburnt hydrocarbons are oxidised to carbon dioxide and water. | CO, NOx, unburnt hydrocarbons |
| Low-sulfur fuel | Sulfur compounds are removed from petrol and diesel at the refinery, so little sulfur dioxide forms when the fuel burns. | SO₂ |
| Gas scrubber (flue gas desulfurisation) | In power station chimneys, waste gases are sprayed with a slurry of calcium carbonate or calcium oxide (an alkali/base), which reacts with acidic sulfur dioxide to form a solid calcium salt (which can be made into gypsum for plasterboard). | SO₂ |
| Particulate filter | A filter in the exhaust of diesel vehicles traps soot particles, which are burned off from time to time. | Particulates |

Typical catalytic converter reactions:

- 2CO + O₂ → 2CO₂
- 2NO + 2CO → N₂ + 2CO₂

Other approaches: better engine design and more complete combustion (more oxygen, less carbon monoxide and soot), using less fuel (public transport, efficient vehicles), and switching to electric vehicles and renewable electricity, which move or remove the emissions.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying catalytic converters remove carbon dioxide. They make more carbon dioxide; they remove CO, NOx and hydrocarbons.
- Saying low-sulfur fuel reduces oxides of nitrogen. NOx comes from nitrogen in the air reacting at high temperatures in the engine, not from the fuel.

### PET bottles and recycling decisions

*Refs: OCR B J258 C4.5.6, C4.5.7; J260 C4.4.5, C4.4.6.*

#### What you need to know

- Describe how PET drinks bottles are reused and recycled for other uses, and explain why this is **viable** (practical and worth doing).
- Evaluate factors that affect decisions to recycle products made from crude oil (plastics) and from metal ores.
- Life cycle assessment and the general idea of reducing, reusing and recycling are in Chemistry 4.10.2.

#### Explained

PET (poly(ethylene terephthalate)) is a polyester used for most clear drinks bottles. It is a **thermosoftening** plastic: it softens when heated and can be remoulded.

**Recycling process.**

1. Bottles are collected and sorted from other plastics (PET has recycling code 1; machines sort by how the plastic reflects infrared light) and by colour.
2. Caps and labels are removed, and the bottles are washed.
3. They are shredded into flakes, which are washed again and dried.
4. The flakes are melted and formed into pellets.
5. Pellets are made into new bottles, or into fibres for fleece clothing, carpets and filling, or into food trays and packaging straps.

**Why PET recycling is viable.**

- It is thermosoftening, so it can be melted and reshaped.
- Drinks bottles are made of one plastic, are used in huge numbers and are easy to identify, so collection gives a large, fairly pure supply.
- Recycled PET needs much less energy than making new PET from crude oil, and saves a finite resource.
- It reduces plastic in landfill and litter.

Its quality falls a little each time it is melted, so it cannot be recycled for ever; it is often "downcycled" into fibres.

**Factors in recycling decisions.**

| Factor | Plastics (from crude oil) | Metals (from ores) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Energy saved | Some, compared with making new plastic | Large: recycled aluminium needs about 5% of the energy of extracting new aluminium by electrolysis |
| Ease of sorting | Hard: many types look alike and mixed plastics are hard to separate | Iron and steel are separated with magnets; aluminium by other methods |
| Quality after recycling | Falls with each cycle | Most metals can be recycled many times with no loss of quality |
| Value of the material | Usually low, so recycling may not pay | Often high (aluminium, copper), so recycling pays |
| Resource saved | Crude oil (finite) | Ore (finite); less mining, quarrying and waste rock |

Other factors: the energy and cost of collecting and transporting waste; whether there is a market for the recycled product; local facilities; and how much material is contaminated with food or mixed with other materials.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying all plastics can be recycled like PET. Thermosetting plastics cannot be melted and remoulded.
- Saying recycling always saves energy. The energy for collection, transport and sorting has to be included.

### Sampling and qualitative or quantitative analysis

*Refs: OCR B J258 C5.2.1, C5.4.1 (both separate science only).*

#### What you need to know

- A **representative sample** has the same composition as the whole material being tested.
- **Qualitative analysis** finds out which substances are present.
- **Quantitative analysis** finds out how much of a substance is present.

#### Explained

Analysts test only a small portion of a material, so the portion must truly represent the whole. For a lake, samples are taken at several places and depths; for a shipment of soil or ore, small samples are taken from many points and mixed. A sample taken only from the surface, or only from one corner, might give the wrong answer.

The sample must also stay unchanged between collection and testing: containers must be clean, sealed and labelled, and stored to stop reactions or evaporation.

Examples:

- Qualitative: flame tests and precipitate tests to identify ions (Chemistry 4.8.3); chromatography to see whether a food colouring contains a banned dye.
- Quantitative: a titration to find the concentration of an acid (Chemistry 4.4.2.5); measuring the mass of a precipitate; an instrument that measures the concentration of a metal ion.

#### Common mistakes

- Thinking a single sample from one place is enough for a large or mixed material.
- Calling a flame test quantitative. It shows which ion is present, not how much.

### Judging the quality of titration data

*Refs: OCR B J258 C5.4.8; J260 C5.3.7.*

#### What you need to know

- The titration method is in Chemistry 4.4.2.5 and the [OCR practical activity groups](../ocr-a-sciences/practical-activity-groups.md) (titration).
- Evaluate a set of titration results: identify the rough titre, concordant titres and outliers; decide which to use in the mean; identify sources of error and say how they affect the result.

#### Explained

- **Concordant** results agree closely, usually within 0.10 cm³. Only concordant titres are used for the mean. The rough (first) titre usually overshoots the end point, so it is left out.
- Results that are close together are **precise**. They are **accurate** if they are close to the true value, which needs correctly calibrated equipment and good technique.
- A burette is read to the nearest 0.05 cm³. Each titre uses two readings (start and end), so it has an uncertainty of about ±0.1 cm³.

Common sources of error and their effect:

| Problem | Effect on the titre |
| --- | --- |
| Air bubble in the burette tip that comes out during the titration | Titre appears larger than it really is |
| Burette rinsed with water, not the acid it is filled with | Acid is diluted, so more is needed: titre too large |
| Overshooting the end point (adding acid too fast near the end) | Titre too large |
| Reading the burette from above or below eye level (parallax) | Reading too high or too low |
| Pipette not filled exactly to the line | Wrong volume of alkali: titre too large or too small |
| Indicator added in large amounts | Indicator can react slightly and shift the end point |

To improve: rinse equipment with the right solutions, remove air bubbles before starting, add acid drop by drop near the end point while swirling, read at eye level against a white background, and repeat until there are at least two concordant results.

#### Worked example

A student records titres of 25.40, 24.70, 24.65, 24.95 and 24.70 cm³ (the first is rough). The concordant titres within 0.10 cm³ are 24.70, 24.65 and 24.70. Mean = (24.70 + 24.65 + 24.70) ÷ 3 = 24.68 cm³. The 24.95 cm³ titre is not concordant and is left out, probably because the end point was overshot.

#### Common mistakes

- Including the rough titre in the mean.
- Averaging all results, including ones that do not agree.
- Saying an air bubble makes the titre smaller. When the bubble leaves the tip, the reading falls without acid reaching the flask, so the recorded titre is too big.

### Check questions

1. A couple both have a parent with cystic fibrosis. Explain how genetic testing could help them make decisions about having children. [3 marks]
2. Give one ethical concern about predictive genetic testing for adults. [1 mark]
3. A doctor sends a throat swab to a laboratory. Describe two ways the laboratory could identify the bacterium and choose an antibiotic. [4 marks]
4. Explain why a slide of bacteria is passed through a flame before staining. [2 marks]
5. Write a balanced equation for the reaction of carbon monoxide with oxygen in a catalytic converter, and explain why low-sulfur petrol reduces acid rain. [3 marks]
6. Describe how a gas scrubber removes sulfur dioxide from power station waste gases. [2 marks]
7. Explain why PET bottles are widely recycled but many other plastic items are not. [3 marks]
8. Suggest why aluminium cans are almost always worth recycling. [2 marks]
9. Explain the difference between qualitative and quantitative analysis, with an example of each. [4 marks]
10. A student forgets to rinse the burette with acid after washing it with water. Explain the effect on the titre. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Test each partner to see if they carry the cystic fibrosis allele (1). If both are carriers, each child has a 1 in 4 chance of having CF (1). They can use this to decide whether to have children, use embryo screening / testing in pregnancy, or prepare for care (1).
2. Any one: anxiety from a result that may never lead to illness; results also reveal information about relatives; use of results by insurers or employers; privacy of genetic data.
3. Any two, 2 marks each (identification and how it helps): culture on agar and examine colonies / microscopy with staining to see the shape and type of bacteria; antibiotic discs on a culture, larger clear zone shows more effective antibiotic; DNA / genome analysis compared with known bacteria; antibody-based test kit.
4. To fix the bacteria to the slide (1) and kill them so they are safe to handle (1).
5. 2CO + O₂ → 2CO₂ (1). Less sulfur in the fuel means less sulfur dioxide is formed when it burns (1); sulfur dioxide dissolves in rainwater to cause acid rain (1).
6. Waste gases pass through a spray of calcium carbonate or calcium oxide (1); this reacts with the acidic sulfur dioxide to form a solid calcium salt (1).
7. PET is thermosoftening, so it can be melted and remoulded (1). Drinks bottles are made of one type of plastic in large numbers, so they are easy to collect and sort (1). Other items are often mixed or thermosetting plastics that are hard to separate or cannot be remelted (1).
8. Recycling aluminium needs far less energy than extracting it by electrolysis (1); aluminium is valuable and can be recycled many times without losing quality (1).
9. Qualitative: identifies which substances are present (1), e.g. a flame test showing lithium ions (1). Quantitative: measures how much is present (1), e.g. a titration to find the concentration of an acid (1).
10. Water left in the burette dilutes the acid (1), so a larger volume is needed to neutralise the alkali: the titre is too large (1).

<!-- source: notes/gcse/ocr-a-sciences/biology-contexts.md -->

## OCR biology contexts: ATP, field sampling, named diseases and food production

The AQA GCSE Biology notes cover almost all of OCR's biology. This file adds only what OCR asks for and AQA does not: ATP as the product of respiration, the full range of field sampling methods (including capture–mark–release–recapture), the extra diseases OCR names, and hydroponics and biological control as ways of producing more food. Read it alongside the AQA sections linked from the OCR maps.

### Overview

Each section below says which OCR outcomes it covers. The rest of each outcome is in the AQA notes: respiration in Biology 4.4.2.1, quadrats and transects in 4.7.2.1, communicable diseases in 4.3.1.1 to 4.3.1.5, plant diseases in 4.3.3.1, and food security and farming in 4.7.5.

#### Board differences

- OCR A (Gateway) J247 and J250: B1.3a (ATP from respiration), B6.1a (field investigation methods), B6.3f (one human infection, one plant disease, STIs). J247 only: B6.2b (agricultural solutions, including hydroponics and biological control).
- OCR B (Twenty First Century) J257 and J260: B4.1.2 (why respiration is continuous), B3.4.2 (field investigation), B2.1.4 and B2.1.5 (named human and plant diseases). J257 only: B6.4.6 (biotechnological and agricultural solutions).
- AQA does not mention ATP or capture–recapture, and names different example diseases. AQA's examples (measles, HIV, tobacco mosaic virus, Salmonella, gonorrhoea, rose black spot, malaria) are still useful for OCR, which accepts any correct example where it does not name one.

### ATP and why respiration never stops

*Refs: OCR A J247/J250 B1.3a; OCR B J257/J260 B4.1.2.*

#### What you need to know

- Respiration is a chemical process that happens all the time in every living cell.
- The energy released by respiration is used to make a molecule called **ATP** (adenosine triphosphate).
- ATP is the cell's immediate energy supply. When it is broken down, it releases energy for processes the cell needs.
- Cells cannot store much ATP, so respiration has to continue all the time to keep making it.
- Aerobic respiration makes much more ATP from each glucose molecule than anaerobic respiration.
- Respiration is exothermic: some of the energy is transferred to the surroundings as heat.

#### Explained

Glucose holds a lot of energy, but in one large package. A cell needs energy in small, ready-to-use amounts, many times a second. Respiration releases the energy from glucose step by step and uses it to join a phosphate group onto ADP (adenosine diphosphate), making ATP.

ADP + phosphate + energy from respiration → ATP

When a process in the cell needs energy, ATP is broken back into ADP and phosphate, releasing the energy where it is needed. The ADP is then recycled and turned back into ATP by respiration.

Processes that use ATP include:

- muscle contraction, so animals can move
- active transport, such as root hair cells taking up mineral ions, or the gut absorbing glucose against a concentration gradient
- building large molecules, such as proteins from amino acids, and starch, glycogen and lipids
- nerve impulses and cell division.

Because a cell holds only a few seconds' supply of ATP, it must respire continuously. If respiration stops (for example, cyanide blocks it), ATP runs out within moments and the cell dies.

Aerobic respiration breaks glucose down completely to carbon dioxide and water, so it releases much more energy and makes far more ATP per glucose (roughly 30 or more) than anaerobic respiration (2). That is why muscles that are respiring anaerobically tire quickly, and why yeast fermenting without oxygen grows slowly.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying respiration "makes energy". Energy cannot be made. Respiration releases energy stored in glucose and transfers it to ATP.
- Confusing respiration with breathing. Breathing moves air in and out of the lungs; respiration is a reaction inside cells, and plants respire day and night.
- Thinking only animal cells respire, or that plants respire only at night. Plant cells respire all the time; in daylight photosynthesis is usually faster, so the plant takes in carbon dioxide overall.

### Field sampling methods

*Refs: OCR A J247/J250 B6.1a; OCR B J257/J260 B3.4.2.*

#### What you need to know

- **Distribution** is where organisms are found; **abundance** is how many there are.
- Plants and slow-moving animals are sampled with quadrats. Random quadrats estimate population size; a transect shows how distribution changes across a habitat. (These are in Biology 4.7.2.1.)
- Mobile animals are estimated by **capture–mark–release–recapture**.
- Animals are collected with pooters, sweep nets, pond nets and pitfall traps.
- **Identification keys** are used to name the organisms found.
- **Indicator species** show the level of pollution.
- Abiotic factors are measured with instruments such as light meters, thermometers and pH probes.
- Results from a sample are **scaled up** to estimate the population of the whole area.

#### Explained

**Capture–mark–release–recapture.** Animals that move about cannot be counted in a quadrat, so a sample is caught, marked harmlessly (for example a small dot of non-toxic paint on a beetle's shell) and released. Later a second sample is caught. Some of these will be marked. If the marked animals have mixed evenly back into the population, the fraction of marked animals in the second sample equals the fraction of the whole population that was marked:

estimated population = (number in first sample × number in second sample) ÷ number marked in second sample

The method only works if:

- no animals are born, die, arrive or leave between the two samples
- the marks do not wash or rub off
- the marks do not make the animals easier for predators to see
- the marked animals have time to mix back into the population.

**Collecting equipment.**

- Pooter: a jar with two tubes. Sucking on one tube draws small insects up the other into the jar.
- Sweep net: swept through long grass to catch flying and crawling insects.
- Pond net: swept through water to catch aquatic animals.
- Pitfall trap: a container sunk into the ground with its rim level with the soil, often with a cover raised above it to keep out rain. Animals walking across the ground fall in. Traps are checked often so the animals can be released.

**Identification keys.** A dichotomous key asks a series of questions, each with two answers (for example "Does it have six legs?"), until only one name is left.

**Indicator species.** Some species survive only in clean conditions; others are found only where there is pollution. For example, many lichens cannot grow where the air contains a lot of sulfur dioxide, and mayfly larvae need water with plenty of dissolved oxygen, while bloodworms and sludge worms tolerate polluted, low-oxygen water.

**Scaling up.** If a sample covers a known area, multiply the mean number per sample by the number of sample areas that fit into the whole habitat.

#### Worked example

A student catches 40 woodlice under logs in a garden, marks each with a dot of paint and releases them. Two days later she catches 50 woodlice, of which 8 are marked. Estimate the population.

estimated population = (40 × 50) ÷ 8 = 2000 ÷ 8 = 250 woodlice.

Ten quadrats of 0.25 m² in a 120 m² field contain a total of 30 daisies. Mean per quadrat = 3. Number of quadrats that fit in the field = 120 ÷ 0.25 = 480. Estimated population = 3 × 480 = 1440 daisies.

#### Common mistakes

- Dividing by the wrong number in capture–recapture. The number of marked animals recaptured always goes on the bottom.
- Leaving too long between samples, so births and deaths change the population, or too short, so the marked animals have not mixed back in.
- Forgetting to convert quadrat area and field area to the same units before scaling up.
- Placing quadrats where it looks interesting instead of using random coordinates, which biases the estimate.

### OCR's named diseases

*Refs: OCR A J247/J250 B6.3f; OCR B J257/J260 B2.1.4, B2.1.5.*

#### What you need to know

OCR A asks for at least one human infection, one plant disease and sexually transmitted infections including HIV/AIDS; its plant examples are tobacco mosaic virus, barley powdery mildew and crown gall. OCR B names influenza, Salmonella, athlete's foot, malaria and HIV/AIDS in humans, and tobacco mosaic virus, ash dieback and crown gall in plants. HIV, Salmonella, malaria and tobacco mosaic virus are in Biology 4.3.1.2 to 4.3.1.5. For each of the others, know the pathogen type, how it spreads, its effects and how it is controlled.

| Disease | Pathogen | Spread | Effects | Control |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Influenza (flu) | Virus | Droplets from coughs and sneezes; touching contaminated surfaces | Fever, aches, tiredness, cough; can be fatal in elderly or ill people | Yearly vaccine, hand washing, staying at home when ill; antiviral drugs in some cases |
| Athlete's foot | Fungus | Direct contact; damp floors, towels and shoes | Itchy, flaking, cracked skin, usually between the toes | Antifungal creams or powders; dry feet well; do not share towels; wear sandals in communal showers |
| Barley powdery mildew | Fungus | Spores carried by wind | White powdery patches on leaves; less photosynthesis, lower grain yield | Fungicides, resistant varieties, crop rotation, removing infected stubble |
| Crown gall | Bacterium (*Agrobacterium tumefaciens*) | Soil bacteria enter through wounds in roots or stem base; contaminated tools | Swollen, tumour-like galls; weakened, stunted plant | No cure: remove and destroy infected plants, avoid wounding, clean tools, do not replant in infected soil |
| Ash dieback | Fungus | Spores from infected fallen leaves blown by wind; movement of infected young trees | Leaves wilt and fall early, dark patches on bark, crown of tree dies back; many trees die | No cure: restrict movement of ash plants, remove infected young trees, breed and plant tolerant trees |

#### Explained

**Influenza** viruses change their surface proteins slightly every year, so antibodies made after last year's infection or vaccine may not recognise this year's virus. That is why flu vaccines are offered every autumn, especially to older people and others at high risk.

**Athlete's foot** fungi feed on dead skin and grow best where it is warm and damp. Keeping feet dry removes the conditions they need.

**Barley powdery mildew** grows on the leaf surface and sends feeding structures into the leaf cells. Covered leaf area cannot absorb light, so less photosynthesis happens and less glucose is available for grain.

**Crown gall** is caused by a bacterium that transfers some of its own DNA into the plant's cells. The plant cells then divide out of control and form a gall. Scientists use this same ability of *Agrobacterium* to carry useful genes into crop plants in genetic engineering.

**Ash dieback** was first confirmed in Britain in 2012 and has since spread through most of the country. It is expected to kill a large majority of ash trees, which affects the many insects, lichens and birds that depend on ash. A small number of trees show tolerance, and these are being used to breed more resistant trees.

**STIs.** HIV (a virus) is in Biology 4.3.1.2 and gonorrhoea (a bacterium) in 4.3.1.3. Both are spread by sexual contact and their spread is reduced by using condoms, testing and treatment. Any correct STI can be given where OCR asks for "sexually transmitted infections".

#### Common mistakes

- Treating a viral disease with antibiotics. Influenza is a virus; antibiotics have no effect on it.
- Saying fungicides "cure" ash dieback. There is no practical cure for trees in woodland; control depends on stopping spread and breeding tolerant trees.
- Calling crown gall a fungal disease because it causes swellings. It is bacterial.

### Hydroponics and biological control

*Refs: OCR A J247 B6.2b; OCR B J257 B6.4.6.*

#### What you need to know

- **Hydroponics** is growing plants without soil, with their roots in a solution of mineral ions.
- **Biological control** uses a living organism (a predator, parasite or pathogen) to reduce the population of a pest.
- Know the advantages and disadvantages of each, compared with using soil, fertilisers and chemical pesticides. (Fertilisers, pesticides and genetic modification are in Biology 4.6.2.4 and 4.7.5.)

#### Explained

**Hydroponics.** The nutrient solution can be adjusted to give each crop exactly the mineral ions it needs, and it is pumped past the roots so they also get oxygen. Crops are usually grown in glasshouses, where temperature, light and carbon dioxide can also be controlled.

Advantages:

- high yields in a small space, all year round
- no soil, so no soil pests, diseases or weeds
- mineral ions are supplied exactly, with less waste
- crops can be grown where the soil is poor or there is little land.

Disadvantages:

- expensive to set up and to run (pumps, heating and lighting use energy)
- needs skilled monitoring of the solution
- a disease in the shared solution can spread quickly to every plant
- plants may need artificial support without soil.

**Biological control.** A natural enemy of the pest is released. Examples: ladybirds eat aphids; a tiny parasitic wasp lays its eggs in glasshouse whitefly larvae, killing them.

Advantages:

- no chemical residues on food or in the environment
- usually affects only the target pest, not other species
- the control organism can breed, so it may not need to be reapplied
- pests do not become resistant in the way they can to pesticides.

Disadvantages:

- slower than a chemical pesticide
- reduces the pest but does not wipe it out
- the introduced species may eat other species, or itself become a pest. For example, cane toads introduced to Australia to control beetles in sugar cane fields spread widely and harmed native wildlife.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying hydroponics needs no fertiliser. The nutrient solution is itself a fertiliser solution.
- Saying biological control "kills all the pests". The aim is to keep the pest population low; if all the pests died, the control organism would die out too.

### Check questions

1. State what ATP is used for in a cell, and explain why cells must respire all the time. [3 marks]
2. Explain why muscle cells respiring anaerobically can work for only a short time. [2 marks]
3. A student catches 24 snails in a garden, marks them and releases them. A week later she catches 30 snails, of which 6 are marked. Estimate the snail population. [2 marks]
4. Give two conditions that must be true for the estimate in question 3 to be valid. [2 marks]
5. Describe how a pitfall trap is set up, and suggest why it should be checked every day. [3 marks]
6. Mayfly larvae are found upstream of a sewage outlet but not downstream. Explain what this suggests. [2 marks]
7. Explain why a person who had flu last winter can catch flu again this winter. [2 marks]
8. Barley powdery mildew covers the leaves of barley plants. Explain why it reduces the yield of grain. [3 marks]
9. Describe how crown gall disease enters a plant and how its spread can be reduced. [3 marks]
10. A grower changes from growing lettuces in soil to hydroponics. Evaluate this change. [4 marks]
11. Give one advantage and one risk of using biological control instead of a chemical pesticide. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Any one use: muscle contraction / active transport / building large molecules such as proteins (1). Cells store very little ATP (1); so respiration must continue to keep making ATP (1).
2. Anaerobic respiration makes far less ATP per glucose (1); and lactic acid builds up, causing muscle fatigue (1).
3. (24 × 30) ÷ 6 (1) = 120 snails (1).
4. Any two: no births, deaths, immigration or emigration between samples; marks do not come off; marks do not affect survival (for example, from predators); marked snails mix back fully into the population.
5. Container sunk in the ground (1); rim level with the soil surface, so animals fall in (1). Checked often so animals can be released before they die or eat each other (1).
6. Mayfly larvae need clean, well-oxygenated water (1); so the water downstream is polluted / low in oxygen because of the sewage (1).
7. The flu virus changes its surface proteins (antigens) (1); so antibodies / memory cells from last year do not recognise it (1).
8. The white fungus covers the leaf, so less light reaches the chloroplasts (1); less photosynthesis (1); less glucose for growth and grain (1).
9. Bacteria in the soil enter through wounds in the roots or stem base (1). Any two: remove and destroy infected plants; avoid wounding plants; clean or sterilise tools; do not replant in the same soil (2).
10. For: higher yield / grown all year in a glasshouse (1); no soil pests, diseases or weeds (1). Against: high costs of set-up and energy (1); a disease can spread through the shared solution (1). Credit a reasoned conclusion within the 4 marks.
11. Advantage: no chemical residue / specific to the pest / no resistance / self-sustaining (1). Risk: control organism may attack other species or become a pest itself / slower to act (1).

## OCR chemistry contexts: empirical formulae, thin layer chromatography and electrolysis with non-inert electrodes

Three pieces of OCR GCSE chemistry are not in the AQA notes: working out empirical formulae, thin layer chromatography (and the choice of solvent and locating agent), and electrolysis with electrodes that take part in the reaction. Everything else in these OCR outcomes is in the AQA Chemistry notes: relative formula mass in 4.3.1.2, paper chromatography and Rf values in 4.8.1.3, and electrolysis with inert electrodes in 4.4.3.

### Overview

#### Board differences

- OCR A (Gateway) J248 and J250: C2.1d (empirical formula), C2.1g (paper and thin layer chromatography), C3.4e (electrolysis with inert and non-inert electrodes).
- OCR B (Twenty First Century) J258 and J260: C3.4.6 and C3.4.7 (empirical formula, with ratio arithmetic), C5.1.6 (chromatographic methods, including aqueous and non-aqueous solvents and locating agents).
- AQA GCSE does not ask for empirical formulae, and its chromatography required practical uses paper only. AQA's electrolysis required practical uses inert electrodes.

### Empirical formulae

*Refs: OCR A J248/J250 C2.1d; OCR B J258/J260 C3.4.6, C3.4.7.*

#### What you need to know

- The **empirical formula** is the simplest whole-number ratio of atoms of each element in a compound.
- The **molecular formula** is the actual number of atoms of each element in one molecule.
- Deduce an empirical formula from the numbers of atoms in a model, a diagram or a molecular formula, and the other way round (what models fit a given empirical formula).
- Calculate an empirical formula from the masses (or percentages by mass) of each element, using relative atomic masses.

#### Explained

To get the empirical formula from a molecular formula, divide all the numbers by their highest common factor.

| Compound | Molecular formula | Empirical formula |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Ethene | C₂H₄ | CH₂ |
| Glucose | C₆H₁₂O₆ | CH₂O |
| Hydrogen peroxide | H₂O₂ | HO |
| Water | H₂O | H₂O |

Ionic compounds and giant covalent structures (such as sodium chloride and silicon dioxide) do not form molecules, so their formula is always the empirical formula: NaCl, SiO₂.

From masses, the method is:

1. Write the mass (or percentage) of each element.
2. Divide each by its relative atomic mass. This gives the relative number of moles of atoms.
3. Divide every answer by the smallest one.
4. If the results are not close to whole numbers, multiply them all by the same small number (2, 3 or 4) to make them whole.
5. Write the formula with the whole numbers as subscripts.

If you know the relative formula mass of the compound, you can go on to find the molecular formula: divide Mr by the mass of one empirical formula unit, and multiply the empirical formula by the answer.

#### Worked example

**From a diagram.** A ball-and-stick model shows a molecule with 4 carbon atoms and 10 hydrogen atoms. Molecular formula C₄H₁₀. Divide by 2: empirical formula C₂H₅.

**From masses.** A 4.0 g sample of an oxide of copper contains 3.2 g of copper. Find its empirical formula. (Ar: Cu = 63.5, O = 16)

| | Cu | O |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Mass (g) | 3.2 | 4.0 − 3.2 = 0.8 |
| ÷ Ar | 3.2 ÷ 63.5 = 0.0504 | 0.8 ÷ 16 = 0.0500 |
| ÷ smallest | 1.01 | 1 |

Ratio 1 : 1, so the empirical formula is CuO.

**Needing a multiplier.** A compound contains 52.9% aluminium and 47.1% oxygen by mass. (Ar: Al = 27, O = 16)

- Al: 52.9 ÷ 27 = 1.96; O: 47.1 ÷ 16 = 2.94
- Divide by 1.96: Al 1, O 1.5
- Multiply by 2: Al 2, O 3. Empirical formula Al₂O₃.

**Molecular formula.** A hydrocarbon has empirical formula CH₂ and Mr = 56. Mass of CH₂ = 12 + 2 = 14. 56 ÷ 14 = 4, so the molecular formula is C₄H₈.

#### Common mistakes

- Dividing the masses by the atomic number, or by the Mr of the element's molecule (O₂ = 32), instead of the relative atomic mass.
- Rounding too early. A ratio of 1 : 1.5 must be doubled to 2 : 3, not rounded to 1 : 2.
- Forgetting to work out the mass of oxygen by subtraction when only the metal and the oxide are weighed.
- Simplifying a molecular formula that cannot be simplified, or writing an empirical formula with fractions.

### Thin layer chromatography and choosing a solvent

*Refs: OCR A J248/J250 C2.1g; OCR B J258/J260 C5.1.6.*

#### What you need to know

- Describe paper chromatography and thin layer chromatography (TLC).
- In TLC the stationary phase is a thin layer of a solid such as silica gel or alumina, spread on a glass, plastic or aluminium sheet. The mobile phase is the solvent.
- Substances separate because they spend different amounts of time dissolved in the mobile phase and stuck to the stationary phase.
- The solvent can be water (aqueous) or another liquid such as ethanol or propanone (non-aqueous). Substances that do not dissolve in water need a non-aqueous solvent.
- Colourless spots are made visible with a **locating agent** or, on some TLC plates, ultraviolet light.
- Rf values (in Chemistry 4.8.1.3) are calculated the same way for TLC as for paper.

#### Explained

**Running a TLC plate.**

1. Draw a pencil line about 1 cm from the bottom of the plate. Pencil does not dissolve and move with the solvent; ink would.
2. Put a small, concentrated spot of each sample on the line, using a capillary tube.
3. Stand the plate in a beaker or tank with solvent below the pencil line, so the spots do not dissolve into the solvent pool.
4. Cover the container so the air inside is saturated with solvent vapour; this stops the solvent evaporating from the plate and gives even movement.
5. Remove the plate when the solvent is near the top, and mark the solvent front in pencil straight away, before it evaporates.
6. Locate the spots and measure the distances.

**Why TLC is often preferred to paper.** The solid layer is more even than paper fibres, so spots are sharper and better separated. TLC runs in a few minutes, needs only tiny samples, and the plate can be used with a wider range of solvents, including ones that would damage paper.

**Choosing the solvent.** A substance only moves if it dissolves in the mobile phase. Food colourings dissolve in water, so water works for them. The ink in a permanent marker does not dissolve in water, so it stays on the baseline in water but separates in ethanol or propanone. The same substance has a different Rf in different solvents, so Rf values are only compared when the solvent (and stationary phase) are the same. A pure substance gives a single spot in every solvent tried; if a sample gives one spot in one solvent, trying a second solvent is a stronger test of purity.

**Locating agents.** Many substances (such as amino acids and sugars) are colourless. After the plate dries, it is sprayed with or exposed to a chemical that reacts with them to give coloured spots. Examples:

- ninhydrin spray, which gives purple spots with amino acids
- iodine vapour, which makes many organic compounds show up as brown spots.

Some TLC plates contain a substance that glows under ultraviolet light; compounds on the plate block the glow and show as dark spots, which are circled in pencil.

#### Worked example

On a TLC plate run in ethanol, the solvent front is 6.0 cm from the baseline. An unknown dye gives spots at 1.5 cm and 4.2 cm. Reference dye X gives one spot at 4.2 cm.

Rf of the upper spot = 4.2 ÷ 6.0 = 0.70. Rf of X = 0.70. The unknown contains two substances; one may be X. To be more confident, run both samples again in a different solvent and check the spots still match.

#### Common mistakes

- Putting the baseline below the solvent level, so the samples wash off into the solvent.
- Measuring to the edge of a spot instead of its centre.
- Calling the solvent the stationary phase. The solvent moves: it is the mobile phase.
- Concluding two substances are the same from one solvent alone. Different substances can share an Rf value in one solvent.

### Electrolysis with non-inert electrodes

*Refs: OCR A J248/J250 C3.4e.*

#### What you need to know

- **Inert electrodes** (graphite or platinum) do not react; they only carry electrons. The products come from the ions in the electrolyte (Chemistry 4.4.3.4).
- **Non-inert (active) electrodes** take part in the reaction. In copper sulfate solution with copper electrodes, the copper anode dissolves and copper is deposited on the cathode.
- This is how copper is purified and how objects are electroplated.
- Compare the results of electrolysing copper sulfate solution with inert and with copper electrodes.

#### Explained

**Copper sulfate with graphite (inert) electrodes.**

- Cathode (−): Cu²⁺ + 2e⁻ → Cu. A pink-brown coating of copper forms.
- Anode (+): oxygen gas is given off (from water or hydroxide ions; sulfate ions are not discharged).
- The blue colour fades as Cu²⁺ ions are used up, and the solution becomes acidic because H⁺ ions and sulfate ions are left behind.

**Copper sulfate with copper (non-inert) electrodes.**

- Anode (+): Cu → Cu²⁺ + 2e⁻. Copper atoms lose electrons and go into solution, so the anode gets lighter. No gas is given off.
- Cathode (−): Cu²⁺ + 2e⁻ → Cu. The cathode gets heavier.
- Copper ions are added to the solution at the anode as fast as they are removed at the cathode, so the concentration and the blue colour stay the same.
- In a perfect cell the mass lost by the anode equals the mass gained by the cathode.

**Purifying copper.** The anode is a block of impure copper; the cathode is a thin sheet of pure copper. Copper from the anode dissolves and is deposited as pure copper on the cathode. Impurities that do not dissolve fall below the anode as a sludge, which can contain valuable metals such as silver and gold.

**Electroplating.** The object to be coated is made the cathode; the anode is the plating metal; the electrolyte contains ions of the plating metal. For example, to silver-plate a spoon, the spoon is the cathode, a silver bar is the anode and the solution contains silver ions. Plating is used to prevent corrosion or to improve appearance.

#### Worked example

A student electrolyses copper sulfate solution with copper electrodes for 20 minutes.

| | Start mass (g) | End mass (g) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Anode | 5.42 | 5.15 |
| Cathode | 5.38 | 5.64 |

Anode change = 5.15 − 5.42 = −0.27 g. Cathode change = 5.64 − 5.38 = +0.26 g. The cathode gains almost exactly what the anode loses, as expected. The small difference can come from copper flaking off the anode or the cathode not being dried fully before weighing.

#### Practical

This is part of OCR's electrolysis practical group (PAG C2 in J248, PAG C1 in J250; see [practical-activity-groups.md](practical-activity-groups.md)). A typical investigation varies the current or the time and measures the change in mass of each copper electrode. Clean the electrodes with emery paper first; rinse the cathode with water and then propanone, and dry it before weighing so the mass of liquid is not included. Copper sulfate solution is harmful and an irritant, so wear eye protection.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying oxygen is given off at a copper anode. With a copper anode, the copper itself is oxidised instead.
- Writing the anode half equation with electrons on the wrong side. Oxidation is loss of electrons: Cu → Cu²⁺ + 2e⁻.
- Saying the solution changes colour when copper electrodes are used. The concentration of Cu²⁺ stays the same.
- Making the object to be plated the anode. It must be the cathode, where metal ions gain electrons and form metal.

### Check questions

1. Give the empirical formula of: (a) C₃H₆ (b) C₂H₆O (c) N₂O₄. [3 marks]
2. 2.4 g of magnesium reacts completely with oxygen to form 4.0 g of magnesium oxide. Find the empirical formula of magnesium oxide. (Ar: Mg = 24, O = 16) [3 marks]
3. A compound is 40.0% carbon, 6.7% hydrogen and 53.3% oxygen by mass. Its Mr is 60. Find its empirical and molecular formulae. (Ar: C = 12, H = 1, O = 16) [4 marks]
4. Give two advantages of thin layer chromatography over paper chromatography. [2 marks]
5. A student runs a sample of permanent marker ink in water and sees no movement. Explain why, and suggest how to improve the experiment. [2 marks]
6. Explain why a locating agent is needed to see amino acids on a chromatogram. [2 marks]
7. Copper sulfate solution is electrolysed with copper electrodes. Write the half equation at each electrode. [2 marks]
8. Explain why the blue colour of the solution fades with graphite electrodes but not with copper electrodes. [3 marks]
9. Describe how electrolysis is used to purify copper. [4 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. (a) CH₂ (1) (b) C₂H₆O (it cannot be simplified) (1) (c) NO₂ (1).
2. Mass of O = 4.0 − 2.4 = 1.6 g (1). Mg: 2.4 ÷ 24 = 0.1; O: 1.6 ÷ 16 = 0.1 (1). Ratio 1 : 1, MgO (1).
3. C 40.0 ÷ 12 = 3.33; H 6.7 ÷ 1 = 6.7; O 53.3 ÷ 16 = 3.33 (1). Ratio 1 : 2 : 1, empirical formula CH₂O (1). Mass of CH₂O = 30; 60 ÷ 30 = 2 (1). Molecular formula C₂H₄O₂ (1).
4. Any two: faster; better / sharper separation; needs smaller samples; more solvents can be used; more sensitive.
5. The ink is insoluble in water, so it does not move with the mobile phase (1). Use a non-aqueous solvent such as ethanol or propanone (1).
6. Amino acids are colourless, so the spots cannot be seen (1). The locating agent (for example ninhydrin) reacts with them to form coloured spots (1).
7. Anode: Cu → Cu²⁺ + 2e⁻ (1). Cathode: Cu²⁺ + 2e⁻ → Cu (1).
8. Blue colour comes from Cu²⁺ ions (1). With graphite, Cu²⁺ ions are removed at the cathode and not replaced, so the colour fades (1). With copper, the anode releases Cu²⁺ ions as fast as they are removed, so the concentration stays the same (1).
9. Impure copper is the anode (1); pure copper is the cathode (1); electrolyte is copper sulfate solution (1); copper dissolves from the anode and pure copper is deposited on the cathode, while impurities fall as sludge (1).

<!-- source: notes/gcse/ocr-a-sciences/practical-activity-groups.md -->

## OCR practical activity groups (PAGs)

OCR does not set fixed required practicals like AQA. Instead it groups the apparatus and techniques every student must use into **practical activity groups (PAGs)**. Each separate science has eight PAGs; Combined Science has sixteen. Schools choose the activities, but written papers can ask about any of the techniques, and about the practicals suggested in the specification. About 15% of exam marks test practical skills.

Most PAGs use the same techniques as an AQA required practical, and the method, risks and analysis are in the AQA notes (each PAG below links to them through the OCR map). This file says what each PAG covers, gives a typical activity, and writes out in full the practicals that AQA does not have.

### Overview

The PAG names are the same in Gateway (A) and Twenty First Century (B). Gateway writes them with the subject letter (PAG B1, PAG C1, PAG P1); the separate Twenty First Century specs write PAG1 to PAG8 inside each subject. The Combined Science specs (J250 and J260) use their own sixteen groups.

| Separate science | Combined Science (J250, J260) |
| --- | --- |
| Biology 1 Microscopy | B1 Microscopy |
| Biology 2 Testing for biological molecules | (part of B3) |
| Biology 3 Sampling techniques | B2 Sampling techniques |
| Biology 4 Rates of enzyme-controlled reactions | B3 Rates of enzyme-controlled reactions |
| Biology 5 Photosynthesis | B4 Photosynthesis |
| Biology 6 Physiology, responses and respiration | (not a separate group) |
| Biology 7 Microbiological techniques | B5 Microbiological techniques |
| Biology 8 Transport in and out of cells | (part of B1 and B3) |
| Chemistry 1 Reactivity trend | (not a separate group) |
| Chemistry 2 Electrolysis | C1 Electrolysis |
| Chemistry 3 Separation techniques | C3 Separation techniques |
| Chemistry 4 Distillation | C2 Distillation |
| Chemistry 5 Identification of species | (not a separate group) |
| Chemistry 6 Titration | (not a separate group) |
| Chemistry 7 Production of salts | C4 Production of salts |
| Chemistry 8 Measuring rates of reaction | C5 Measuring rates of reaction |
| Physics 1 Materials | P1 Materials |
| Physics 2 Forces | P2 Forces |
| Physics 3 Motion | P3 Motion |
| Physics 4 Measuring waves | P4 Waves |
| Physics 5 Energy | P5 Energy |
| Physics 6 Circuit components | P6 Circuits |
| Physics 7 Series and parallel circuits | (part of P6) |
| Physics 8 Interactions of waves | (not a separate group) |

#### Board differences

- AQA lists fixed required practicals; OCR lists apparatus and techniques and suggests activities. The AQA required practical linked from each OCR map row uses the same techniques.
- OCR B outcome B4.1.5 (J257 and J260) asks students to describe investigations into how different substrates affect the rate of respiration in yeast, which is written out in Biology PAG 6 below.
- The OCR combined groups B2 to B5 and C1 to C5 are numbered differently from the separate groups, so always check which list a question means. This file uses the separate-science names, with the combined code in brackets.

### Biology PAGs

*Refs: OCR A J247 PAG B1 to B8; J250 PAG B1 to B5. OCR B J257 PAG 1 to 8, B4.1.5; J260 PAG B1 to B5.*

#### What you need to know

| PAG | Techniques | Typical activity | AQA notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 Microscopy (comb. B1) | Light microscope, slide preparation, staining, labelled drawings, magnification | Onion epidermis or cheek cells; root tip squash for mitosis | Biology 4.1.1.5 |
| 2 Testing for biological molecules | Water bath, food test reagents | Benedict's, iodine, biuret and ethanol (emulsion) tests | Biology 4.2.2.1 |
| 3 Sampling techniques (comb. B2) | Quadrats, transects, capture–recapture, keys, measuring abiotic factors | Daisies in a field; distribution across a path or shore | Biology 4.7.2.1; capture–recapture in [biology-contexts.md](biology-contexts.md) |
| 4 Enzymes (comb. B3) | Water bath, pH buffers, timing a colour change or gas volume | pH or temperature and the rate of amylase or catalase | Biology 4.2.2.1 |
| 5 Photosynthesis (comb. B4) | Measuring gas production, light intensity | Light distance and bubbles from pondweed | Biology 4.4.1.2 |
| 6 Physiology, responses and respiration | Measuring reaction time, pulse; respirometers; ethical use of organisms | Reaction time; exercise and pulse rate; yeast respiration | Biology 4.5.2.1, 4.4.2.1 |
| 7 Microbiological techniques (comb. B5) | Aseptic technique, culturing on agar, measuring inhibition zones | Antibiotic or antiseptic discs on a bacterial lawn | Biology 4.1.1.6 |
| 8 Transport in and out of cells | Measuring mass or length change; serial dilution | Potato cylinders in sugar or salt solutions | Biology 4.1.3.2 |

#### Explained

**PAG 6: Respiration in yeast with different substrates (OCR B B4.1.5).** Yeast respires sugars and releases carbon dioxide. The rate of carbon dioxide release shows the rate of respiration.

1. Make up a yeast suspension and equal-concentration solutions of different sugars (for example glucose, sucrose, fructose, lactose).
2. Mix equal volumes of yeast suspension and one sugar solution in a boiling tube. Stand it in a water bath at a fixed temperature (about 35 °C) for a few minutes so it reaches that temperature.
3. Fit a bung and delivery tube. Either collect the gas in an upturned measuring cylinder or gas syringe, or count bubbles through water in a set time.
4. Measure the volume of gas in a fixed time (for example 5 minutes). Repeat for each sugar, and do a control with water instead of sugar.

Control: the same mass of yeast, volume and concentration of sugar, temperature and time. Yeast usually respires glucose and fructose fastest, sucrose more slowly (it must first be broken down) and lactose hardly at all (yeast lacks the enzyme to digest it).

**PAG 6: Respirometers.** A respirometer measures oxygen uptake by small organisms such as woodlice or germinating seeds. The organisms sit on a gauze above a chemical that absorbs carbon dioxide (such as soda lime or potassium hydroxide). As they use oxygen, the gas volume in the tube falls, and a drop of coloured liquid in a capillary tube moves towards them. Distance moved per minute (or volume, from the tube's cross-section) gives the rate. A second tube with glass beads instead of organisms is the control, to correct for temperature and pressure changes.

**PAG 6: Exercise and pulse rate.** Measure resting pulse (beats in 15 seconds × 4), exercise for a fixed time (such as 2 minutes of step-ups), then measure pulse every minute until it returns to resting. The recovery time is a measure of fitness.

**Ethics with living organisms.** Use the smallest number of animals needed, keep them in their habitat conditions, handle them gently, return them to where they were found, and never use vertebrates for experiments that harm them. Students' own data (pulse, reaction time) are used only with their consent.

#### Common mistakes

- Not letting the tubes reach the water-bath temperature before starting timing.
- Forgetting the control tube in a respirometer, so temperature changes are mistaken for oxygen uptake.
- In yeast experiments, changing the mass of sugar instead of keeping the concentration the same.
- Counting bubbles when bubbles are different sizes; a gas syringe gives more accurate volumes.

### Chemistry PAGs

*Refs: OCR A J248 PAG C1 to C8; J250 PAG C1 to C5. OCR B J258 PAG 1 to 8; J260 PAG C1 to C5.*

#### What you need to know

| PAG | Techniques | Typical activity | AQA notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 Reactivity trend | Mixing reagents safely; measuring temperature change or gas | Metals with acid; displacement reactions | Chemistry 4.4.1.2 |
| 2 Electrolysis (comb. C1) | Electrochemical cells, inert and non-inert electrodes, testing gases | Electrolysis of aqueous salts; copper electrodes | Chemistry 4.4.3.4; non-inert electrodes in [chemistry-contexts.md](chemistry-contexts.md) |
| 3 Separation techniques (comb. C3) | Filtration, evaporation, crystallisation, chromatography | Rock salt purification; paper or thin layer chromatography | Chemistry 4.1.1.2, 4.8.1.3 |
| 4 Distillation (comb. C2) | Simple and fractional distillation, heating safely | Pure water from salty water; separating ethanol and water | Chemistry 4.10.1.2 |
| 5 Identification of species | Flame tests, precipitate tests, gas tests | Identifying ions in unknown salts | Chemistry 4.8.2, 4.8.3 |
